    "transcript": "00:00 [Intro music and graphics showing 'Coaching TOPS' and 'Crown Council Community' and 'Skill of the Week, August 20, 2024'.] \n00:06 [Steven J Anderson and Stuart Anderson appear on screen, both smiling. Six seconds to make a first impression.\n00:15 **Steven J Anderson:** That's right. [Steven nods, smiles] And study after study, it's been proven that the first [Steven gestures with right hand, index finger pointing up] and lasting impressions [Steven gestures widely with both hands, palms facing each other, then clasps them lightly] are made in the first [Steven holds up five fingers on his right hand, then adds his thumb to make six] six seconds. [Steven holds up one index finger] That's it. [Steven gestures widely with both hands, open palms] Six seconds. [Steven smiles, nods slightly, looks at Stuart]\n00:28 **Stuart Anderson:** That is not very long. [Stuart smiles, shakes his head slightly]\n00:31 **Stuart Anderson:** And that's because the brain processes impressions in a split second, [Stuart leans forward slightly, looking directly at the camera, serious expression] which triggers an emotional response. The first six seconds set an emotional stage for the entire appointment and may determine how long the patient relationship actually lasts. [Stuart nods, maintains eye contact with the camera, expression still serious but engaged]\n00:48 **Steven J Anderson:** So, [Steven gestures with both hands, palms up, open posture, smile returns] let's list six things as fast as we can [Steven gestures quickly with both hands, as if listing points] that you can do in the first six seconds [Steven emphasizes 'six seconds' with distinct hand gestures, counting on fingers briefly] for a great first impression. So, Stuart, for every one I mention, you have to come up with another one. We'll go back and forth. [Steven makes a back-and-forth motion with his hands, indicating a volley] We'll see how we can do how six and as fast as we can. So you ready? [Steven smiles, leans forward slightly, directs gaze at Stuart]\n01:06 **Stuart Anderson:** Ready. [Stuart nods, smiles]\n01:07 **Steven J Anderson:** Smile. [Steven gives a broad, genuine smile]\n01:08 **Stuart Anderson:** Uh, greet the patient by name. [Stuart leans forward slightly, a serious, focused expression then a quick, small smile]\n01:10 **Steven J Anderson:** Anticipate the patient's arrival. [Steven gestures slightly with his right hand, then nods, maintaining a positive expression]\n01:12 **Stuart Anderson:** Express appreciation. [Stuart nods, looks directly at the camera, earnest expression]\n01:15 **Steven J Anderson:** Shake their hand. [Steven makes a clear hand-shaking gesture, smiles broadly]\n01:17 **Stuart Anderson:** Nice. Yeah. [Stuart nods, smiles] How about look them in the eye? [Stuart points towards his eyes with both index fingers, then looks at Steven]\n01:20 **Steven J Anderson:** Bam! [Steven points his right index finger upwards, then quickly pulls it back, smiling brightly] Alright, that was six. So it doesn't take long [Steven gestures with right hand, open palm] and it doesn't take that much to make a great first impression in six seconds or less. [Steven claps his hands together once for emphasis, broad smile]\n01:30 **Steven J Anderson:** So make them glad they're there in your office in the first six seconds. [Steven makes a fist with his right hand for emphasis, then transitions to open palms, gesturing outwardly] Don't wait. It's first six seconds of every patient encounter. [Steven smiles, nods, maintains a direct gaze]\n01:40 **Stuart Anderson:** Nice. Thanks, Steven. Well, what about in contrast? [Stuart raises eyebrows, leans forward, a mischievous smile forming] Are you ready for the alternative challenge? Let's name six things as fast as we can to completely destroy a great first impression. [Stuart maintains the mischievous smile, points his index finger as if challenging Steven, vocal tone is light and playful]\n01:49 **Steven J Anderson:** Really? [Steven laughs loudly, shakes his head playfully] Okay. We're gonna we're gonna flip it. [Steven points downward with both hands, then gestures outward, smiling broadly and still laughing slightly]\n01:53 **Stuart Anderson:** Okay. You go first. [Stuart nods, smiles, touches his chin thoughtfully, still in a playful mood]\n01:54 **Steven J Anderson:** All right. Ignore the patient. [Steven smiles, laughs softly, glances away from the camera for a moment]\n01:56 **Stuart Anderson:** Very good. All right. Never use their name. [Stuart points his index finger playfully, a smirk on his face]\n01:58 **Steven J Anderson:** Avoid eye contact. [Steven shakes his head slightly] Don't look at 'em. [Steven laughs quietly, avoids eye contact briefly]\n02:01 **Stuart Anderson:** Ask, [Stuart makes a mock-serious, slightly exaggerated expression, raising his eyebrows, speaking with a slightly sarcastic tone] \"Are you new?\"\n02:04 **Steven J Anderson:** Oh, or how about this one? Ask, [Steven gestures with open palms, a playful, slightly incredulous expression, speaks with a humorous, rising inflection] \"May I help you?\"\n02:08 **Stuart Anderson:** How about run late and never apologize? [Stuart smiles, nods, shrugs slightly, still playing along with the humor]\n02:11 **Steven J Anderson:** Bam! [Steven makes a playful punching motion with his right fist, laughs] Okay, there's six. So it doesn't make, [Steven gestures widely with hands, open palms, smiling] it doesn't take much to get off to a really, really bad start. [Steven shakes his head slightly, gestures widely again, smiling]\n02:19 **Steven J Anderson:** So, here's the challenge. Become a master of the first six seconds [Steven gestures widely with both hands, then brings them together, palms facing each other, indicating a small, precise space, his expression becoming more earnest] with every patient. Your first six seconds. [Steven points to Stuart with his right hand, then back to himself, emphasizing the individual responsibility] So, no matter your position in the practice, take responsibility for making your six seconds [Steven gestures again as if holding a small, precise object, then points to himself] with every patient, those first six seconds, positive ones that set the stage [Steven gestures with both hands, palms facing up, moving them upwards and outwards as if building something, his tone is uplifting and inspiring] for all the many seconds to follow. [Steven maintains a serious, encouraging expression, looking directly at the camera]\n02:42 **Stuart Anderson:** Nice. Thanks, Steven. And thanks for the six-second reminder. [Stuart smiles, nods]\n02:46 **Steven J Anderson:** You bet. Now, go master the skill [Steven gestures with right hand, open palm, then points playfully towards the camera] in six seconds. [Steven gives a final broad smile and nod.]",

{
  "vimeo_id": "1005872359",
  "title": "September 3, 2024: Fairness and Policy",
  "source": "SOW Pages 2-5",
  "post_url": "/products/crown-council-team-training-library/categories/2149126468/posts/2180880352",
  "video_url": "https://player.vimeo.com/video/1005872359?badge=0&autopause=0&player_id=0&app_id=58479",
  "video_analysis": "```json\n{\n    \"transcript\": \"[Music starts, intro graphic shows 'Skill of the Week', 'September 3, 2024'. Music fades out.]\\n\\n[Stuart Anderson, left, smiles and gestures broadly with both hands, leaning forward slightly.]\\n**Stuart Anderson:** All right, everybody, welcome to the Crown Council Skill of the Week.\\n\\n[Steven J Anderson, right, smiles, leans forward slightly.]\\n**Steven J Anderson:** All right, Stu, today we're gonna talk about law enforcement.\\n\\n[Stuart Anderson smiles widely, raises eyebrows, head tilts, lets out a small laugh.]\\n**Stuart Anderson:** Yikes, law enforcement in dentistry, Steve?\\n\\n[Steven J Anderson smiles, nods, gestures to affirm, then widens eyes and gestures broadly with hands for emphasis, then settles back.]\\n**Steven J Anderson:** That's right. You got it. [pauses] So, you would not intentionally [gestures widely] want to bring in the image of law enforcement into your practice as part of your patient service strategy... [pause, takes a deep breath]\\n\\n[Steven J Anderson gestures widely with both hands, eyebrows raised, shakes head slightly for emphasis, then lowers hands and looks serious/concerned.]\\n**Steven J Anderson:** ...but that is exactly what happens in practices every day.\\n\\n[Stuart Anderson leans forward, serious but curious expression, nods.]\\n**Stuart Anderson:** We gotta hear more about this, Steve. How is this possible?\\n\\n[Steven J Anderson smiles, leans forward, makes explanatory hand gestures.]\\n**Steven J Anderson:** All right, so it's all about word association. So let's play a little game here. A word association game. I'm gonna give you, I'm gonna give you a word, and then you're gonna give me back all the words that immediately come to mind by association. Are you ready?\\n\\n[Stuart Anderson nods, smiling.]\\n**Stuart Anderson:** I'm ready.\\n\\n[Steven J Anderson leans forward slightly, direct eye contact.]\\n**Steven J Anderson:** Policy.\\n\\n[Stuart Anderson leans back slightly, thinks with furrowed brow, then smiles and quickly rattles off words with active hand gestures.]\\n**Stuart Anderson:** Oh, uh, I think about police, and penalties, and restrictions, and rules, and regulations. How's that?\\n\\n[Steven J Anderson nods, smiles, gestures openly with both hands.]\\n**Steven J Anderson:** All right. Words, so this is a great example of how words [opens hands widely for 'evoke'] evoke an emotional response. Words matter. [nods, emphasizes with hand gesture] Right? Because they're tied to emotions. Now some words just evoke a negative emotional response. [leans forward, points finger] Like the word 'policy'.\\n\\n[Steven J Anderson gestures widely, counting off points on his fingers, a slight frown and head shake expressing exasperation.]\\n**Steven J Anderson:** And yet it gets used all the time. The financial policy, the appointment policy, the infection control policy, the payment policy. Now every business has to have policies, but reciting the policy by name to a patient [shakes head emphatically, gestures open with hands] is poor patient service. You don't want that negative emotion associated with what you do.\\n\\n[Stuart Anderson nods in agreement, places hand on chest, shakes head, smiles wryly, then slight head shake.]\\n**Stuart Anderson:** I agree, Steve. I actually cannot think of a single time that anyone has ever recited a policy to me and made me happy. A-It's a huge turn-off.\\n\\n[Steven J Anderson nods emphatically, points finger to make a point, gestures open, then leans in, adopts a softer, mock-apologetic tone and facial expression, gestures to himself as if apologizing.]\\n**Steven J Anderson:** That's right. So in addition, the word 'policy' is many times accompanied by an apology. 'Stuart, I'm so sorry, but our policy is...' [Steven J Anderson makes a face of resigned apology.]\\n\\n[Steven J Anderson raises eyebrows, gestures open to Stuart, looks curious.]\\n**Steven J Anderson:** How does it, how does it make you feel?\\n\\n[Stuart Anderson shakes head, scrunches face slightly in strong negative emotion.]\\n**Stuart Anderson:** It's terri- I feel- makes me feel horrible.\\n\\n[Steven J Anderson raises hands slightly, then nods to acknowledge, then leans forward, points finger for emphasis, makes a \"better way\" gesture, direct eye contact.]\\n**Steven J Anderson:** All right, so I'm not saying you don't have policies. You gotta have 'em. What I am saying is there is a better way to communicate them to your patients.\\n\\n[Stuart Anderson nods, smiles, leans forward, open hand gesture.]\\n**Stuart Anderson:** Nice. So let's say I'm a patient, Steve, and I ask, 'Can I pay the week after my appointment?' [Stuart's tone is slightly hesitant for the patient's question, slight head tilt.]\\n\\n[Steven J Anderson nods, shakes head slightly, gestures dismissively, then leans forward with a warm smile, makes a \"this is the way\" gesture, speaks clearly and slowly, emphasizing key words.]\\n**Steven J Anderson:** Right. So instead of reciting the policy, right? 'Our policy is, Stuart...' [Steven shrugs dismissively] Here's what we recommend. You would say, 'Stuart, in order to be fair to all of our patients, payment's due at the time of service.'\\n\\n[Steven J Anderson points finger up for 'key phrase', emphasizes the phrase, gestures broadly with open hands, confident smile.]\\n**Steven J Anderson:** So the key phrase is: 'In order to be fair to all of our patients,' here's what we do.\\n\\n[Stuart Anderson nods, smiles, leans back, thoughtful nod, then more assertive nod.]\\n**Stuart Anderson:** Nice. Yeah, it's pretty hard to argue against fairness. We all believe that things should be fair.\\n\\n[Steven J Anderson nods, gestures widely with one hand, points finger, then makes a chopping gesture for 'get rid of it', then open hands, confident smile.]\\n**Steven J Anderson:** That's right. So that phrase can be used in almost every situation where you might be tempted to use the word 'policy'. Get rid of it, but replace it with the phrase, 'in order to be fair to all of our patients,' here's what we do.\\n\\n[Stuart Anderson nods, smiles, leans forward, gestures open.]\\n**Stuart Anderson:** Nice, Steve. That's much better patient service.\\n\\n[Steven J Anderson nods, smiles, points forward confidently, then a final broad gesture as if sending Stuart off to practice.]\\n**Steven J Anderson:** You got it. Now go master the skill.\\n\\n[Music swells, outro graphic shows 'Crown Council Community' and 'Steven J Anderson'.]\\n\",\n    \"tone_analysis\": {\n        \"overall_tone\": \"The overall tone is highly **encouraging and educational**, delivered with a **professional yet conversational and warm demeanor**.     "transcript": "[Music plays with logos for \"Coaching TOPS\" and \"Crown Council Community\"]\n[00:00:06] [Music fades, video shows split screen of Stuart Anderson on left, Steven J Anderson on right. Both are smiling and looking at each other.]\n[00:00:06] **Stuart Anderson:** Welcome to the Skill of the Week where we feature a specific skill to increase your case acceptance and patient service success. [smiles, nods slightly, enthusiastic tone] Hey, Steve!\n[00:00:15] **Steven J Anderson:** Well, it might not. [smiles, head shakes slightly, playful tone]\n[00:00:17] **Stuart Anderson:** Oh. [slight head tilt, eyebrows raised, curious] You mean we're not, are we not doing that today? What do you mean? [slight chuckle, still curious]\n[00:00:20] **Steven J Anderson:** You said it would increase their case acceptance success. Well, it might not if they don't implement it. [leans forward, smiles, points with finger playfully, then gestures widely with hands, still playful]\n[00:00:25] **Stuart Anderson:** Oh yeah, that's a great kickoff. Thanks for that, Steve. [smiles, light chuckle, nods, appreciative] Yeah.\n[00:00:29] **Steven J Anderson:** You're welcome. [smiles broadly, slight head tilt] So, Stuart, you never know when you're going to encounter what we call an opposite sorter. [gestures widely with both hands, eyebrows raised for emphasis, enthusiastic]\n[00:00:38] **Stuart Anderson:** Yes. [smiles, nods, listening intently] Someone who always takes the opposite point of view, Steve, is that what we're talking about? [nods, leans slightly forward, curious, smiling]\n[00:00:43] **Steven J Anderson:** That's right. So, you say black, I say white. You say yes, I say no. You say hello, I say goodbye. [gestures with hands, palms up, demonstrating opposition, animated facial expressions] Some people are just wired that way. And everybody knows somebody like this, or more people. They can be very, very difficult, especially as a patient, unless you know how to communicate with them. [leans forward, becomes more serious and emphatic, uses hands to illustrate \"difficult,\" then shifts to a more helpful tone for \"communicate\"]\n[00:01:02] **Stuart Anderson:** Yeah. [nods in agreement]\n[00:01:03] **Stuart Anderson:** Okay, Steve. Well, let's say that a patient remarks that the treatment is too expensive, that it probably won't work, or that they just have to fix it again because it's gonna break. [nods, maintains eye contact, serious and understanding tone, mimicking patient's potential negative outlook]\n[00:01:12] **Steven J Anderson:** All right. So, when you pick up the signals that you're dealing with an opposite sorter, like that, they're coming up with every reason why it won't work. [nods, points finger as if picking up signals, then gestures widely to indicate all reasons, serious but helpful tone] Just present things from their point of view. So, I would say, \"Stuart, let me share with you what's worked for a lot of our patients in in a similar situation to yours. Now, just because it worked for them, does not mean it will be right for you. But it might be something you want to consider.\" [leans forward, open hand gestures, calm and reassuring tone, emphasizes key phrases, then becomes more direct with \"does not mean,\" but softens with \"might be\"]\n[00:01:41] **Stuart Anderson:** Nice. [smiles, nods, impressed] So, if I'm an opposite sorter, because you told me it might not be right for me, I'll automatically jump to the opposite side and start thinking of all the ways it would work for me. [smiles broadly, gestures with hands, eyebrows raised, demonstrating understanding and the patient's likely thought process, slightly playful]\n[00:01:53] **Steven J Anderson:** That's right. So, the next time you encounter an opposite sorter who disagrees with everything you say, just agree with them. Tell them, \"You know, you might be right. It might not be right for you.\" [nods, points, emphatic, then gestures openly, smiles, demonstrates the verbal skill with a slightly sarcastic but effective tone]\n[00:02:08] **Stuart Anderson:** But it might. [smiles, points to himself, playful]\n[00:02:09] **Steven J Anderson:** Yeah, that's exactly how an opposite sorter will respond. They're always going to respond in contrary to whatever you say. So, just take their side and they'll take yours. [nods, smiles, gestures widely, confident, then leans forward with a concluding gesture]\n[00:02:20] **Stuart Anderson:** Nice. Thanks, Steve, for the insight into opposite sorters and the verbal skills to properly communicate with them. [smiles, nods, appreciative, slight head tilt]\n[00:02:27] **Steven J Anderson:** You are welcome. [smiles broadly, looks up briefly, then back to Stuart] It may not work for everyone. But, you might give it a try next time you come across an opposite sorter. You may be very pleasantly surprised at their response. Then again, you might not. [leans back, gestures widely and playfully, ending with a smile and a shrug, maintaining a humorous tone]\n[00:02:42] **Steven J Anderson:** Now, go master the skill. [nods, assertive, encouraging]\n[00:02:44] [Video ends]",

  "video_analysis": "```json\n{\n    \"transcript\": \"[Music playing] \\n[0:06] Stuart Anderson: Alright everybody, welcome to the Skill of the Week. [leans forward slightly, a serious, almost conspiratorial tone, then shifts to lightheartedness] Uh, Stuart, can you give me a discount? [playful, direct, challenging tone]\\n[0:15] Stuart Anderson: Um, we don't do discounts, Steve. [slight head shake, maintains a friendly smile, firm but gentle]\\n[0:17] Steven J Anderson: Well, my last dentist gave me a discount because my family is so big. [leans in, mock-whining tone, slight shrug]\\n[0:20] Stuart Anderson: Ugh, these are the (worst) conversations, Steve. I hate 'em. [rolls eyes slightly, exaggerated discomfort, then laughs]\\n[0:24] Steven J Anderson: [laughs softly] (Exactly). [nods, leans back slightly]\\n[0:25] Steven J Anderson: So, they're uncomfortable at best. [nods, more serious, reflective tone]\\n[0:29] Steven J Anderson: So, what's the alternative? [gestures with hands] What's the system for addressing discount requests? [leans forward slightly, rhetorical question, engaging tone]\\n[0:34] Steven J Anderson: Alright, so here, it's a simple phrase. [nods, opens hands slightly, confident, ready to present solution]\\n[0:38] Steven J Anderson: So, I would say, Stuart, if you asked me, I would say, Stuart, thank you so much for (asking). [gestures with right hand, direct address, appreciative and calm tone]\\n[0:42] Steven J Anderson: You know, Doctor Jones made the decision a long time ago to (set) our fees (fairly), [nods, deliberate, emphasizes words with slight pauses, calm and confident tone] so we would (never) have to (apologize) for the (quality) of the work you (receive). [emphatic, uses hands to frame a space, serious, slightly leaning forward for impact]\\n[0:56] Steven J Anderson: So, your (investment) to get that (beautiful smile) you've always (wanted) is fifteen hundred, you know, whatever the fee is. [gestures widely to encompass, warm, reassuring tone, then transitions to practical pricing]\\n[1:02] Steven J Anderson: (Right?) That's... that's the way to phrase it. [smiles, nods, confident, conclusive tone]\\n[1:05] Steven J Anderson: So we address it and we (move on). [right hand gesture, firm, conclusive tone]\\n[1:07] Stuart Anderson: Nice. I love it. {
  "vimeo_id": "1017548244",
  "title": "October 8, 2024: How You Treat the One",
  "source": "SOW Pages 2-5",
  "post_url": "/products/crown-council-team-training-library/categories/2149126468/posts/2181809228",
  "video_url": "https://player.vimeo.com/video/1017548244?badge=0&autopause=0&player_id=0&app_id=58479",
  "video_analysis": "```json\n{\n    \"transcript\": \"[music starts and fades] \\n[0:06] Stuart Anderson: [smiles broadly, energetic] Hey everybody, and welcome to the Skill of the Week! \\n[0:10] Steven J Anderson: [raises right index finger briefly, then gestures widely with both hands, smiling] Today, we are going to talk about *sheep*. \\n[0:15] Stuart Anderson: [leans forward slightly, curious, smiles slightly] *Sheep*, Steve? *Sheep*? [gestures with hands open, palms up, eyebrows raised] What in the world do *sheep* have to do with patient service? \\n[0:19] Steven J Anderson: [smiles, nods, hands come together] Alright, this is what we call the *Law of 90 and 9*. \\n[0:24] Steven J Anderson: So, everybody remembers the parable of the shepherd and the lost sheep, right? [nods] So, the shepherd has 100 sheep, one wanders off, [gestures with right hand moving away from the body] so the shepherd leaves the 99 [gestures widely to encompass a group, then brings hands together] to find the *one* [raises right index finger to emphasize 'one'] and bring it back to the fold. [brings hands together, palm to palm, then gestures softly] \\n[0:40] Stuart Anderson: [nods, smiles, hands clasped, leans forward slightly] Yeah, nice. It's a lesson all about the *value of the one*. Every *one* is important: every patient, every team member, every supplier, everybody. \\n[0:50] Steven J Anderson: [leans forward, hands gesturing emphatically and rapidly, eyes wide with enthusiasm] So, the *other* moral to the story is the *message* that the shepherd *sent* to the 99. We often focus on the *one*, but by focusing on the *one*, the *99* know that had *they* been the one to get lost, the shepherd would have come for *them* as well. \\n[1:10] Steven J Anderson: [leans back slightly, hands open, palms up, then clasps hands, then gestures outward, emphasizing words] So, how you treat the *one* sends a message to all the 99 of *their* value and how they ultimately will be treated. That's why we call it the Law of 90 and 9. \\n[1:26] Stuart Anderson: [nods, hands clasped, leans forward slightly] Nice, Steve. Yeah, we've done some exit interviews with patients leaving the dental offices... \\n[1:35] Stuart Anderson: [nods, gestures slightly with hands, maintaining a thoughtful expression] ...to find out what they notice most about the office, what was their impression, what had the biggest impact on them. And we originally thought patients would notice the decor or the cleanliness of the office and the friendliness of the team. \\n[1:46] Steven J Anderson: [leans forward, shakes head emphatically, smiles broadly, hands out, palms up, with a \"ta-da\" gesture] Nope. None of that. \\n[1:50] Steven J Anderson: [gestures emphatically with both hands, then points between his two hands, then spreads them wide, speaking with increasing energy] And this is this is what surprised us, that instead, the overwhelming, most frequent response was how the dental team treated *each other* - including the doctor. [points between hands] Think about it. How the patient observes the team interacting with each other is an indication of how people are generally valued in the practice. \\n[2:16] Stuart Anderson: [nods, hands clasped, leans forward, serious and direct] If any team member is treated with disrespect by another team member, the patient takes note. That mental note goes something like this: \\\"If they treat *each other* like that, *I* will eventually be treated the same way if I keep coming back to this practice long enough.\\\" \\n[2:31] Steven J Anderson: [nods, gestures with hands emphatically, raising voice for emphasis on 'each other' and 'directly', then gestures with open palms as if revealing something] That's right. It's kind of an *inside peek* at what's really going on in the practice and how you value relationships. \\n[2:49] Stuart Anderson: [nods, smiles, hands clasped, concluding tone] Nice. Yeah, I guess we could say, Steve, that just *be nice* to each other. People are watching. \\n[2:55] Steven J Anderson: [nods, raises index fingers for emphasis on 'one', then gestures widely with open palms] That's right. It only takes *one* to send a powerful message to *everyone*. \\n[3:02] Stuart Anderson: [smiles, nods, hands clasped] Alright, thanks for the patient service reminder about *sheep* and the Law of 90 and 9. \\n[3:07] Steven J Anderson: [smiles broadly, hands open, palms up, then gestures widely with a final, encouraging sweep] Happy to do it. [shrugs shoulders lightly] Hard to do, [shakes head slightly, gestures outwards with both hands] we don't want to do it. [gestures openly with both hands, then pulls hands back towards chest] Or, treatment's easy with our convenient and comfortable sedation choices. [gestures widely with both hands, palms up]\n1:33 I'm on board. [smiles, nods, hands clasped] It all sounds so easy to me.\n1:36 It is. [smiles, nods] All right, so here's the challenge: [leans forward, points finger at camera] Today, and every day, find as many ways [gestures widely with both hands] as you can use the word easy [points finger at camera to emphasize 'easy'] in your patient conversations. Or, Stuart, we have some easy payment options.     "transcript": "[Music plays] \n[0:00] [Title card: TOPS COACHING COMMUNITY Steven J Anderson]\n[0:06] Stuart Anderson: [Smiles widely, leans into camera, enthusiastic] Alright, hey everybody, and welcome to the Skill of the Week. \n[0:12] Steven J Anderson: [Holds dental floss to mouth, actively flossing, looking at Stuart] Hey, Stuart. \n[0:13] Stuart Anderson: [Confused, then smiles] Oh, uh, hey, Steve, you- I mean, are you ready to get started? Uh, recording? You good? \n[0:17] Steven J Anderson: [Continues flossing, muffled speech] Yeah, just, just flossing. \n[0:18] Stuart Anderson: [Chuckles, leans in slightly] Oh, yeah, I see that. \n[0:21] Steven J Anderson: [Stops flossing, pulls floss from mouth, looks at camera, serious but engaging tone, gestures emphatically] In a nationwide survey, an overwhelming number of patients responded that they do not want their dentist or hygienist to tell them to floss. \n[0:30] Stuart Anderson: [Nods, smiles slightly] Don't... \n[0:32] Steven J Anderson: [Puts floss back in mouth, exaggerates flossing motion, emphatic and humorous] Tell me... \n[0:35] Steven J Anderson: [Removes floss, pauses for effect, still emphatic] ...to floss. \n[0:37] Stuart Anderson: [Gestures with open hands, curious tone] Steve, I thought flossing was very important, though. \n[0:40] Steven J Anderson: [Drops floss to lap, leans slightly forward, serious and instructional tone, gestures with hands] It is. The keyword in the survey was 'tell'. [Emphasizes 'tell'] In other words, patients don't want anyone to tell them what to do. [Points finger for emphasis] They don't want to be told to floss, they don't want to be told to brush, they don't want to be told to use the water pick or tell them to do anything. Today, no one wants to be told what to do. [Emphasizes 'no one', gestures widely] \n[1:03] Stuart Anderson: [Smiles, nods] Including 16-year-olds, Steve. \n[1:05] Steven J Anderson: [Chuckles] Exactly. \n[1:07] Stuart Anderson: [Becomes serious, hands clasped] So, what's a dental team to do if patients don't want to be told what to do? \n[1:12] Steven J Anderson: [Leans forward, serious and direct, raises finger] So the key is one of the most powerful words in persuasion: [Pauses for emphasis] the word 'because'. [Emphasizes 'because' with a slight vocal lift] We don't want to be told what to do unless it includes the reason why. [Gestures with open hands] So, giving a reason produces significantly higher responses in persuading patients to do something than when no 'because' or reason is given. So, use the word 'because'. [Gestures to emphasize the word 'because'] \n[1:39] Stuart Anderson: [Nods, leans in, hands clasped, demonstrating the skill] Nice, Steve. So, it is very important to floss because it can disrupt the recolonization of bacteria that causes gum disease. [Smiles, nods] \n[1:49] Steven J Anderson: [Eyes widen, enthusiastic, gestures with a thumbs up] Oh, that was good! It's all about the 'because' or the reason. \n[1:54] Stuart Anderson: [Serious, direct gaze, leans forward slightly] So, there is a reason to include that magic word in your patient conversation because it is much more persuasive and will help your patients make the right decision. [Nods, smiles] \n[2:05] Steven J Anderson: [Enthusiastic, smiles widely, gestures with both hands] That's right! You got it. So, now it's about time to wrap up the Skill of the Week because patients are waiting. [Winks slightly, lighthearted] \n[2:12] Stuart Anderson: [Smiling, hands together] Thanks, Steve, for the tip and the great word 'because' to add to our case acceptance vocabulary. \n[2:17] Steven J Anderson: [Begins flossing again, enthusiastic, gestures with one hand] You got it! So, now go master the skill because it will help you hear 'yes' more often from your patients. [Smiles] \n[2:26] Steven J Anderson: [Continues flossing, winks] Back to flossing. \n[2:29] [Music fades]",

{
  "vimeo_id": "1026562122",
  "title": "November 5, 2024: 4 Questions for Case Acceptance",
  "source": "SOW Pages 2-5",
  "post_url": "/products/crown-council-team-training-library/categories/2149126468/posts/2182516203",
  "video_url": "https://player.vimeo.com/video/1026562122?badge=0&autopause=0&player_id=0&app_id=58479",
  "video_analysis": {
    "transcript": "Alright everybody, welcome to the Skill of the Week. [smiles, open gesture with hands] \nAlright, we're gonna take a quick trip to business school today. [smiles, gestures with right hand, index finger raised, then opens hand]\nYeah, business school, alright. [smiles, nods, hands clasped] \nSo, every business school teaches a simple format to analyze different businesses when they do what they call case studies. [gestures with both hands, palms up, then points right index finger] So, the analysis is the acronym S W O T, SWOT, which stands for Strengths, Weaknesses, Opportunities, and Threats. [gestures widely with both hands, emphasizing each word; points to himself with both thumbs at 'Strengths'; points left with right hand at 'Weaknesses'; gestures upward with both hands at 'Opportunities'; gestures downwards with both hands at 'Threats'] \nNice. So if a dental team were analyzing where their practice is today, they could ask themselves what are the practice's strengths, what are its weaknesses, what are the opportunities it has for growth, and what are its competitive threats? [nods, gestures with right hand, then left, then both hands up, then both hands down, mirroring Steven's gestures] \nGot it. So, to translate that into patient service, the same formula applies. [smiles, gestures widely with both hands, then points right index finger] Let's say you are a new patient, Stuart, [points to Stuart] and I could ask you, first, what are the things that you liked about your previous dentist, okay? So that's the strength. [smiles, gestures with right hand outward] \nUm, they had, they had very convenient hours. [looks up, then nods, smiles slightly] \nExcellent. [smiles broadly] Next question would be, what are the things you didn't like, okay? Weaknesses. [gestures with hands open, then points downwards with right hand] \nUm, they always ran behind. [nods, smiles slightly] \nIf you had the ideal smile and oral health, what would that look like for you? [gestures expansively with both hands, palms up, then gestures with right hand outward] That's the opportunity. [nods] \nOkay. So, white and straight smile. [nods, smiles slightly] \nAnd then finally, would there be anything standing in your way of moving in that direction? Threats. [gestures with both hands outward, then brings them together, then points downward with both hands] \nUm, just finding the right dentist that has the time to do it all. [shrugs slightly, smiles] \nSo there you go. In short order, I now know what you like, [gestures with right thumb] what we can do better in terms of service than what you've had in the past, [points right index finger] what you want for the health and appearance of your smile, [gestures widely with both hands, palms up] and the potential obstacles standing in the way of getting it done, the potential threats. [gestures downward with both hands, counting on fingers with each point] \nNice. Pretty easy, Steve. Four, four simple questions. [smiles, nods] \nSo, you can use it with an existing patient to improve the level of service and advance their level of care. [gestures with right hand, then left, then both hands moving upward] So, let's say this time, Stuart, you're a patient who's been coming to the office for five years. [points to Stuart] So, I could ask you these four questions. [holds up four fingers] First, strengths. You've been coming to our office for five years. What are the things that we're doing that you like, that we should make sure we continue to do? [gestures with both hands, palms up, then points to Stuart, then gestures with both hands outward] \nNice. Well, Dr. Steve, every time I come in, it's like I've never left. Everyone remembers me. It is great. [smiles, gestures with hands open] \nOkay, here's the next question, weaknesses. What else, Stuart, could we be doing that would make your visits even better? [gestures with both hands, palms up, then points to Stuart, then gestures widely with both hands, palms up] \nHm. I, I can't think of any anything. Everything, everything's great. [shakes head slightly, smiles] \nOkay. [gives a thumbs up] \nNext question, opportunities. When you think about your smile and the health of your mouth, Stuart, what things are the most important to you? What do you want it to be like five, ten years from now? [gestures expansively with both hands, palms up, then points to Stuart, then gestures widely with both hands, palms up, then extends both hands forward] \nI'd love, I'd love my smile, my teeth to be white and and straight. [nods, smiles slightly] \nAnd then finally, the threats. What's standing in the way of moving in that direction? [gestures with both hands outward, then brings them together, then gestures downward with both hands] \nJust a plan. Let's figure it out, let's do it. [nods, gestures with right hand, then both hands together] \nNow, with that very brief formula, we're moving pretty fast here, S W O T, you can find out a lot and move the patient relationship to a much higher level. [gestures with right hand, then counts on fingers, then gestures widely with both hands, moving upward] \nNice. SWOT. It's easy, I love it, and it has so many applications. [smiles, gestures with hands open] \nYou can use it in almost any situation. [gestures widely with both hands, palms up] \nNice. So use the SWOT formula to improve your patient communication, patient service, and case acceptance. It's a proven practice that you can use with any patient. Thanks, Steve. [nods, smiles, gestures with right hand, then open hands] \nYou're welcome, Stuart. [smiles, gestures with right hand] Now, go master the skill. [gestures with both hands in a 'go get 'em' motion]",

[nods] A lot easier to make payment arrangements and schedule the next appointment while the patient is still in the office, for sure. \n[00:33] Stuart Anderson: [smiles broadly] Nice. Yeah, not in the parking lot. [leans forward, expressive] If we're talking about where in the office is the best place, I would imagine it's chairside in the treatment room. \n[00:41] Steven J Anderson: [smiles broadly, nods] You got it. So there's an inseparable connection between physiology [points to his head with his index finger] and psychology. [gestures widely with both hands again] Right? So our body position impacts how we think. So for example, [leans slightly forward] if I've been comfortably sitting in the dental chair for over an hour, and then you suddenly get me up and walk me down the hall [gestures to the side with one hand, then raises both hands again] to presumably have a conversation about scheduling or financial arrangements, the moment my feet hit the floor, my mind automatically goes out the door [gestures sweeping out and up with both hands] and onto the myriad of other things I have to get done. I may be there physically, but mentally, I'm gone. [nods emphatically, then shrugs slightly] \n[01:19] Stuart Anderson: [leans forward, nods] Yeah, great point. So it only makes sense to get as much done chairside as possible while I'm comfortable and focused in the dental chair. \n[01:29] Steven J Anderson: [nods, smiles] That's right. So, when possible, take everything to the patient. Don't make the patient come to you. [gestures with open hands, emphasizing 'come to you'] And while that's not always possible, it is the ideal and the ideal is what we strive for wherever possible. \n[01:46] Stuart Anderson: [nods, serious expression] Yeah. Steven, in some states like California, the law prohibits discussing financial options in the treatment room, so a quiet consult room would be second best. \n[01:57] Steven J Anderson: [nods] Correct. For everyone else, [raises one hand, palm up, with an upward motion] keep it chairside. \n[02:02] Stuart Anderson: [nods, smiles] Nice. Yeah, so if you ever have a question about where the best place is to do financial arrangements, scheduling, and almost everything else related to patient care, the best is chairside. \n[02:13] Steven J Anderson: [smiles, gestures enthusiastically with one hand, then both] Now go master the skill in the treatment room. [Nods slightly, maintains smile]\\nSteven J Anderson: And so it is in dentistry.\\nStuart Anderson: [Smiles broadly, gestures to himself with both hands, palms up] I know they always want me to be on time for my dental appointments, Steve.\\nSteven J Anderson: [Claps hands together once for emphasis, then gestures with hands open] That matters, and so much more. One of our Crown Council mentors, Dan Pink, has written an entire book entitled, 'When: The Scientific Secrets of Perfect Timing'. [Gestures with both hands, open palms, as he articulates the book title clearly and deliberately]\\nSteven J Anderson: We usually spend most of our time planning *what* we should be doing, and not enough on *when* we should be doing it to get the best results. [Gestures with hands, palms up, to emphasize 'what', then brings hands together and points with index fingers to emphasize 'when', serious expression but still engaging]\\nSteven J Anderson: For example, most surgical mistakes are made in the afternoon when the surgical team is tired. When do you offer morning or late afternoon appointments? When you start promoting the Smiles for Life campaign. There are so many *whens* to consider.   "video_analysis": "```json\n{\n    \"transcript\": \"[Music starts, upbeat, energetic] \\n[00:06] Stuart: [smiles warmly, hands clasped] Alright everybody, welcome to the Skill of the Week. \\n[00:09] Steven: [smiles, nods slightly] Hello, Stewart. \\n[00:10] Stuart: [smiles broadly, chuckles lightly, head tilted] Steve, what's up with your sign? \\n[00:13] Steven: [holds up a white sign with \"Entropy Happens!\" written on it, smiles] It's my Entropy Happens! sign. \\n[00:16] Stuart: [nods, slight chuckle, brings hand to chin, thoughtful look] Entropy happens, very good. So uh, you've got some explaining to do. Please tell us. \\n[00:21] Steven: [leans forward slightly, expressive face, opens mouth widely as he speaks] Well, Entropy does happen. I get it.   "video_analysis": "```json\n{\n    \"transcript\": \"[Music playing, upbeat and energetic]\\n[0:06] Stuart Anderson: All right, everybody, welcome to the Skill of the Week.\\n[0:08] Steven J Anderson: [Claps hands once, leans forward, eyes wide with enthusiasm] All right. If you had the chance to play racquetball, [gestures widely with both hands, palms up, as if offering options] or pickleball, [leans forward slightly, eyebrows raised, with a curious, slightly mischievous expression] which would you choose?\\n[0:17] Stuart Anderson: Hmm. [contemplative look, slight smile] Steve, I am, I am good with either one. Both very fun. [nods, smiles warmly]\\n[0:20] Steven J Anderson: [Nods, smiles] Okay. [gestures with open hands] Have you ever thought about the difference between the two?\\n[0:24] Stuart Anderson: No. No.\\n[0:26] Steven J Anderson: So, like in pickleball, [leans forward, holds hands up as if framing a small ball, then quickly pushes them apart] if you hit the ball too far to the right or the left, the ball goes out of bounds, it's out of play. [sweeps right hand outwards and away, as if indicating something leaving a boundary]\\n[0:33] Stuart Anderson: Right.\\n[0:34] [Visual transition to an image of a person holding a pickleball paddle and ball, with players in the background on a court. Then transitions to a man and woman playing racquetball in a closed court, showing the walls.]\\n[0:34] Steven J Anderson: [Voiceover] But in racquetball, because the game is played in a closed room with four walls, a wall on every side, if you hit the ball too far to the right or the left, it just bounces back onto the court, and the ball is still in play. [Visual returns to Steven, he brings hands together, palms facing each other, then pushes them together slightly, indicating containment] The game continues. So, [raises hands, palms out, in a \"ta-da!\" gesture, then smiles broadly] I like racquetball.\\n[0:52] Stuart Anderson: [Laughs, smiles broadly] Nice. I'd never thought about it that way, but you are very right, Steve. Very good.\\n[0:57] Steven J Anderson: [Nods, smiles, leans forward, brings hands together, fingers curled, as if holding a small object to make a point] All right. Now, in the same way, too often we hit the figurative ball to the patient in a way that gives too many chances for it to go out of bounds. [gestures widely with both hands, sweeping outwards, a slight look of mock frustration] In other words, we're playing pickleball. [gestures widely, palms up] The problem is created with questions like this: [leans in, lowers voice slightly, with a direct, instructive tone] Stuart, when would you like to come in?\\n[1:16] Stuart Anderson: Uh, how about Saturday, Steve?\\n[1:18] Steven J Anderson: No, no, no. We're not open Saturday. [shakes head, waves hands in dismissal, a slight frown of mock disappointment]\\n[1:21] Stuart Anderson: Oh, okay. Uh, Friday afternoon?\\n[1:23] Steven J Anderson: No, no, no. We're not open Friday afternoon. In fact, we're not open at all. [shakes head, throws hands up in exasperation, then breaks into a wide smile and chuckles slightly]\\n[1:27] Stuart Anderson: [Laughs, leans in, points finger forward for emphasis, smiling] That is the tennis ball going out of bounds, Steve.\\n[1:32] Steven J Anderson: Correct. [nods, smiles] So, now, [leans forward intently, raises both index fingers for emphasis, then opens hands slightly as if presenting options] let's play racquetball instead by giving you a choice. So I would say, Stuart, would it be more convenient for you to come in on Tuesday [points down with left index finger] or Thursday? [points down with right index finger]\\n[1:47] Stuart Anderson: Very good. Yeah, Steve. I can make Tuesdays work. [nods, smiles]\\n[1:49] Steven J Anderson: [Gestures widely with open hands, then makes fists and brings them together, then spreads them slightly] Most patients will choose between options presented. So when you give patients a chance to say yes, like, which day do you want to come in, you run the risk of the ball going out of bounds. [sweeps hand outwards again, replicating earlier gesture] But when you give them a choice [makes fists again, brings them together], from the options that are available, the ball [brings fists together then spreads hands slightly, palms facing inward, showing containment] stays in play. It stays in bounds. [nods, smiles]\\n[2:12] Stuart Anderson: Nice. [smiles, nods] Yeah, I guess you could say that we have the opportunities to play racquetball instead of pickleball in just about every interaction. If you want to keep the patient service, case acceptance ball in play, give the patient a choice between two options, either one of which will work. [nods, points finger forward firmly]\\n[2:29] Steven J Anderson: You got it. [nods, smiles widely, points index finger up for emphasis, then makes fists, then opens hands widely with palms facing out, a triumphant gesture] So, give them a choice and keep the case acceptance ball in play. Now, go master the skill, go play racquetball. [points forward decisively, then gestures widely with enthusiasm] Give 'em a choice.\\n[2:42] [Music outro]\\n\",\n    \"tone_analysis\": {\n        \"overall_tone\": \"The overall tone is highly energetic, enthusiastic, and encouraging.
